I'm going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, and I just can't keep operating like this.\n\nDoctor: Were you there when your cousin died?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was. Or, at least, I got there right at the end. But honestly, that's part of what's so frustrating—and frankly, frightening—to me right now. \n\nI can account for all the funeral arrangements and the dozens of calls I made the next day perfectly. But the actual night... parts of the police call and what exactly happened when I first reached the hospital are simply blank. I just don't have the memories. And as someone who relies on keeping track of details, having those pieces missing is just not like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you been avoiding reminders of that night?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean... I haven't gone out of my way to avoid anything. I'm still keeping to my usual routine, going into the office, talking to family. I haven't tried to stay away from the hospital area or anything like that. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm actively avoiding reminders.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you felt emotionally numb or unable to feel close to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have. During the prayer gatherings we've had, and even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I really noticed it. I found myself behaving properly and doing what was expected, but... I just didn't feel any warmth or relief inside. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone quiet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I haven't tried to stay away from the hospital area or anything like that. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm actively avoiding reminders.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you felt emotionally numb or unable to feel close to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have. During the prayer gatherings we've had, and even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I really noticed it. I found myself behaving properly and doing what was expected, but... I just didn't feel any warmth or relief inside. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have upsetting memories of that night been popping into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have. It tends to happen at odd moments. Like... if I hear a phone ring late at night, or if I catch the glare of headlights on the highway. \n\nWhen that happens, a really sharp picture of the hospital corridor just pushes into my mind, or I'll suddenly hear the police officer's voice again. It catches me off guard, and I usually have to just pause for a second to steady myself before I can move on with whatever I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you been feeling constantly on edge or watchful, like you can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I notice it most when I'm driving. I find myself constantly checking the mirrors, watching the speed of any trucks around me, and looking for places where I could pull off the road. Even on the familiar routes I take to the office every day, my body just stays tight. It's almost as if I'm just waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since your cousin died?\n\nPatient: It's been about three weeks now. All of this has been going on since that night... the night he died in the highway collision.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I’m still showing up for the most essential meetings at the office. But I’ve had to delegate my usual community obligations and... well, shorten my workdays. I'm just not as steady or as effective with people as I normally am right now.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have there been moments when you felt as if the crash or the hospital scene were happening again right then, not just that you were remembering it?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel like it's actually happening right then. I definitely think about it a lot, but I always know I'm just... remembering it. It's not like I lose touch with where I am or feel like I'm back in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Did you directly witness the highway collision itself?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't. I wasn't anywhere near the highway when it happened. I just... I got the call about it afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's almost as if I'm just waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since your cousin died?\n\nPatient: It's been about three weeks now. All of this has been going on since that night... the night he died in the highway collision.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I’m still showing up for the most essential meetings at the office. But I’ve had to delegate my usual community obligations and... well, shorten my workdays. I'm just not as steady or as effective with people as I normally am right now.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have there been moments when you felt as if the crash or the hospital scene were happening again right then, not just that you were remembering it?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel like it's actually happening right then. I definitely think about it a lot, but I always know I'm just... remembering it. It's not like I lose touch with where I am or feel like I'm back in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Did you directly witness the highway collision itself?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't. I wasn't anywhere near the highway when it happened. I just... I got the call about it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you been feeling intense sadness or hopelessness most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm definitely sad, but I wouldn't say it's an intense sadness or hopelessness all day long. I'm just... dealing with the grief, you know? But it's not overwhelming me to the point of feeling hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms continued for a full month or longer since your cousin died?\n\nPatient: No, not quite a full month. It's been going on for about three weeks now, ever since the night my cousin died in that highway collision. I'm just not as steady or as effective with people as I normally am right now.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have there been moments when you felt as if the crash or the hospital scene were happening again right then, not just that you were remembering it?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel like it's actually happening right then. I definitely think about it a lot, but I always know I'm just... remembering it. It's not like I lose touch with where I am or feel like I'm back in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Did you directly witness the highway collision itself?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't. I wasn't anywhere near the highway when it happened. I just... I got the call about it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you been feeling intense sadness or hopelessness most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm definitely sad, but I wouldn't say it's an intense sadness or hopelessness all day long. I'm just... dealing with the grief, you know? But it's not overwhelming me to the point of feeling hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms continued for a full month or longer since your cousin died?\n\nPatient: No, not quite a full month. It's been going on for about three weeks now, ever since the night my cousin died in that highway collision. So we're just at the three-week mark.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you often felt dazed or emotionally stunned, like you were in a fog?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced that. I mean, I'm upset, obviously, but my head is clear. I wouldn't say I've been in a fog or feeling dazed at all. I've still been able to focus and handle my day-to-day things at work just fine.\n\nDoctor: When you got the call, were you told that your cousin might die or had died from the crash?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure. When I got the call about the accident... I don't remember exactly if they said he had already passed away right then, or if they just said he might. I really just don't recall the exact words they used.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I haven't tried to stay away from the hospital area or anything like that. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm actively avoiding reminders.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you felt emotionally numb or unable to feel close to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have. During the prayer gatherings we've had, and even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I really noticed it. I found myself behaving properly and doing what was expected, but... I just didn't feel any warmth or relief inside. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have upsetting memories of that night been popping into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have. It tends to happen at odd moments. Like... if I hear a phone ring late at night, or if I catch the glare of headlights on the highway. \n\nWhen that happens, a really sharp picture of the hospital corridor just pushes into my mind, or I'll suddenly hear the police officer's voice again. It catches me off guard, and I usually have to just pause for a second to steady myself before I can move on with whatever I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you been feeling constantly on edge or watchful, like you can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I notice it most when I'm driving. I find myself constantly checking the mirrors, watching the speed of any trucks around me, and looking for places where I could pull off the road. Even on the familiar routes I take to the office every day, my body just stays tight. It's almost as if I'm just waiting for something bad to happen.\n\nDoctor: How long has it been since your cousin died?\n\nPatient: It's been about three weeks now. All of this has been going on since that night... the night he died in the highway collision.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have these symptoms made it harder for you to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, I’m still showing up for the most essential meetings at the office. But I’ve had to delegate my usual community obligations and... well, shorten my workdays. I'm just not as steady or as effective with people as I normally am right now.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have there been moments when you felt as if the crash or the hospital scene were happening again right then, not just that you were remembering it?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel like it's actually happening right then. I definitely think about it a lot, but I always know I'm just... remembering it. It's not like I lose touch with where I am or feel like I'm back in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Did you directly witness the highway collision itself?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't. I wasn't anywhere near the highway when it happened. I just... I got the call about it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you been feeling intense sadness or hopelessness most of the day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm definitely sad, but I wouldn't say it's an intense sadness or hopelessness all day long. I'm just... dealing with the grief, you know? But it's not overwhelming me to the point of feeling hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms continued for a full month or longer since your cousin died?\n\nPatient: No, not quite a full month. It's been going on for about three weeks now, ever since the night my cousin died in that highway collision. So we're just at the three-week mark.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin died, have you often felt dazed or emotionally stunned, like you were in a fog?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced that. I mean, I'm upset, obviously, but my head is clear. I wouldn't say I've been in a fog or feeling dazed at all. I've still been able to focus and handle my day-to-day things at work just fine.\n\nDoctor: When you got the call, were you told that your cousin might die or had died from the crash?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure. When I got the call about the accident... I don't remember exactly if they said he had already passed away right then, or if they just said he might. I really just don't recall the exact words they used.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms start right away on the night your cousin died?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. The first day or two after I got the news was... well, a bit of a blur. So I couldn't tell you if everything started that exact night, or if it took a little while to set in. I just don't know the exact timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you know whether these sleep problems and intrusive memories began within the first month after your cousin died?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I know I'm dealing with them now, but trying to pin down exactly when the sleeping issues and those memories actually started... I just don't know if it was within that first month or a bit later. I really don't have a clear timeline in my head.\n\nDoctor: In the first hours after you learned about your cousin’s crash, did you feel detached from yourself or from what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you. Like I said, those first few hours are just... they're so fuzzy. I don't specifically remember feeling detached or like I was outside of myself. I was mostly just in shock, I think. But honestly, I don't have a clear enough memory of that night to say for sure if I felt that way or not.\n\nDoctor: Did your sleep problems first begin within one month after your cousin died?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. Like I was saying, it's hard for me to put a date on when the sleep issues actually started. I just know I'm struggling with it now, but whether it was in that first month... I couldn't say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
